Build Your Own Harness

ChatGPT
Honestly? I think this could be the best decision you've made with this project.
But I want to approach it differently this time. Not another massive ecosystem that takes weeks to
configure before it can actually do anything.
Let's build your own AI harness, from the ground up.
And I mean genuinely yours. Not a fork of DSH, not a reskinned OpenCode, not 40 plugins glued together.
The vision: Your own AI harness
Think of something combining the best ideas from:
DeepSeek Harness — agent orchestration and coding workflows.
OpenCode — model abstraction, tools and extensibility.
OpenClaw — persistent agents and autonomous workflows.
Hermes Agent — skills, memory and learning.
Claude Code — sophisticated coding agent workflows.
Codex — delegation, isolated workspaces and parallel agents.
But with our own architecture and decisions.
1. Core architecture
I'd split the harness into eight independent systems.

harness

The important part: these should be separate systems, not one enormous application.

2. What I would prioritize
I'd build in this order:
Phase 1 — Foundation
Project structure and architecture.
Model/provider abstraction.
Streaming inference.
Tool-calling support.
Conversation/session management.
Basic CLI.
Phase 2 — Intelligence
Agent runtime.
Model routing.
Component Responsibility
Model Engine Connect to virtually any model/provider, local or cloud.
Router Select models based on task, capability, availability and cost.
Agent Runtime Execute agents, manage tool calls and coordinate work.
Memory Engine Persistent memory, project context, RAG and knowledge graphs.
Skills System Discover, create, install and execute skills.
Tools/MCP Give agents access to external applications and environments.
Orchestration Subagents, parallel execution, task queues and delegation.
Interface CLI initially, with a desktop/web interface eventually.

4. What I'd do differently this time
I'd establish three rules:
1. Working software over impressive architecture. Every phase must produce something usable.
2. Research before implementation. Study how existing harnesses solve individual problems, then
implement our own solutions.
3. No premature complexity. Don't build GraphRAG, multi-agent swarms or a plugin marketplace
before the basic agent works reliably.
Before we start, I need to establish five things.
I don't want to assume the old vision is still your vision.

You mentioned Rust, and I think it's worth taking seriously.

Discord's engineering decisions are relevant, but our requirements are different.
Here's what I'd investigate:
Category Decision

Memory Extremely ambitious, persistent, interconnected
Learning Continuous improvement through verified experience

Autonomy Highly autonomous, with configurable permissions

===== PAGE 17 =====
17 / 352
How to test the finished product.
Whether the result meets expectations.
B. The memory engine
I want to go considerably further than conventional RAG.
My vision:
Episodic memory: What happened during previous sessions?
Semantic memory: What does Lain know?
Procedural memory: How does Lain accomplish tasks?
Project memory: What is happening in each project?
User memory: Preferences, decisions and working habits.
Skill memory: Verified solutions and reusable techniques.
Relationship memory: Connections between projects, concepts and discoveries.
I'd also investigate knowledge graphs, GraphRAG, memory consolidation and automatic memory
maintenance.
And importantly, memory needs provenance. Lain must distinguish verified information from assumptions.
C. Self-improvement
You mentioned Hermes, and yes, that's an excellent system to study.
Hermes Agent is particularly interesting because of its persistent memory and skill creation capabilities.
I want Lain to eventually:
1. Attempt a task.
2. Record the outcome.
3. Evaluate what worked.
4. Identify reusable knowledge.
5. Create or improve a skill.
6. Test the skill independently.
7. Store verified results.
8. Apply the knowledge in future sessions.
Not uncontrolled self-modification.
We need testing, versioning, approval policies and rollback.

However, finding opportunities and actually acquiring paying customers are different problems. We need to

4. The money-making strategy
I have a proposal.
Instead of choosing between making $100 quickly and building something that eventually generates
thousands, we pursue two tracks.
The short-term track teaches us what people actually pay for.
The long-term track gives us something we can scale.
I'd also like to investigate which opportunities have:
No upfront investment.
No paid API requirements.
Low maintenance costs.
Minimal customer acquisition expenses.
Realistic demand.
Potential for automation.
One important consideration: because you're under 18, some payment processors, marketplaces and
business accounts require an adult account holder or guardian involvement. We'll account for that from the
beginning.
Track Objective Examples
Short-term First $50–$100 Websites, automation services, digital products
Long-term Recurring income SaaS, AI software, automated businesses, ecommerce

5. More questions: Round 3
Now I want to get into some deeper architectural and philosophical decisions.
These are important because they will influence almost everything we build.
1. How much freedom should Lain have?
Imagine you give Lain a complicated task and go to sleep.
Should it be allowed to:
Create files and modify projects?
Install dependencies?
Execute terminal commands?
Create new agents?
Generate plugins?
Modify its own configuration?
Improve its own skills?
Research and pursue business opportunities?
I'd suggest a permission system with configurable autonomy levels rather than one global permission
setting.
2. Should agents have individual identities?
For example:
Agent Specialty
Main Agent General intelligence and orchestration
Architect System design
Developer Software engineering
Researcher Research and information gathering
Designer UI/UX

5. How should Lain handle failures?
Suppose an agent spends 15 minutes building something, encounters an error and can't fix it.
Should it:
Retry with the same model.
Switch models.
Delegate to another agent.
Research the error.
Roll back and try a different approach.
Ask for human intervention.
I'd want an intelligent recovery system with bounded retries and proper failure reporting.

My biggest ambition for Lain is this:
Eventually, you should be able to open a terminal, tell Lain what you're trying to accomplish, and have it
figure out how to get there.
Whether that's developing software, researching a business, creating a product, managing projects or
building entirely new capabilities.
And it should get better at doing those things over time.
That's the product we're designing.

3. The architecture is becoming clearer
Here's the conceptual architecture I'd like to investigate:
Level Permissions
0 — Observe Read information, inspect projects and research.
1 — Workspace Create and modify files in authorized workspaces.
2 — Execute Run commands and tests within restrictions.
3 — Integrate Use APIs, MCP, browser and desktop tools.
4 — Autonomous Combine capabilities and execute approved workflows independently.
5 — Unattended Work independently within explicitly configured boundaries.

Agents collaborating on a project shouldn't accidentally overwrite each other's work.
B. Context intelligence
Lain should understand context consumption, automatically summarize old information, preserve important
details and transfer work between sessions.
C. Model intelligence database
Every model gets an independently maintained profile with actual benchmark results, availability, context
limits, pricing and reliability.
D. Execution recovery
When something fails, Lain should determine whether to retry, switch models, delegate, research, restore a
checkpoint or ask for help.
No infinite retry loops.
E. Business intelligence
A dedicated system that researches opportunities, evaluates demand, develops prototypes and helps us
validate potential products.
I want this developed alongside the actual harness.

2. How many agents should Lain be able to run simultaneously?
2–4
5–10
10–25
As many as hardware and provider limits permit.
I'd implement dynamic concurrency rather than a fixed number.
3. Should Lain have a local-first architecture?
Meaning:
All memory stored locally by default.
Local model support.
Local project indexing.
Local execution.
Cloud models only when permitted.
No mandatory external account or service.
I'd strongly recommend this, especially considering your previous privacy and cost requirements.
4. How should Lain manage its own development?
Imagine Lain is tasked with improving its own router.
Should it:
1. Create an isolated development branch.
2. Analyze the existing implementation.
3. Propose changes.
4. Implement them.
5. Run benchmarks and tests.
6. Compare results against the previous version.
7. Present the changes for approval.
8. Merge only when authorized.

6. One last architectural principle
I want to establish this now:
Lain must never depend on its own existence to remain useful.
Its plugins should work independently where possible. Its memory should be exportable. Its model
integrations should be replaceable. Its projects shouldn't become trapped inside proprietary formats.
If we ever decide to replace the entire agent runtime, we shouldn't lose everything else.
That's how we avoid recreating the problems of your previous ecosystem.
I'm also interested in tracking new agent frameworks, memory research, model-routing techniques and
computer-use projects as this develops.
We're not writing the first line of code just yet. I want these decisions settled, then we'll conduct the
serious research and produce Lain's actual engineering blueprint.

a consumer PC.

SGLang Efficient serving, scheduling and advanced inference.

OH, this is an interesting find for Lain's Model Foundry.
I looked through the repository. There's an important distinction, though: this isn't an alternative to
llama.cpp. It's a GPU hardware optimization project.
What it does
The project, nv-uefi-unlocker , is a Rust-based NVIDIA GPU modification project.
Its stated purpose is to unlock additional Tensor compute performance on certain NVIDIA GPU generations.
The repository describes:
NVIDIA GPU compute-performance modifications.
UEFI-level implementation.
Support for various RTX 20, 30 and 40 series cards.
Performance measurements across different compute workloads.
A GPL-2.0 license.
Its performance claims are project-reported, not independent benchmarks.

Where it fits into Lain
I'd separate hardware-level GPU modifications from ordinary inference optimization.

benchmarking.

modifications.
One architectural addition

Its responsibilities would include:
Hardware capability detection.
GPU and CPU utilization monitoring.
VRAM and RAM profiling.
Model compatibility analysis.
Inference performance benchmarking.
Thermal and resource monitoring.
Identifying software optimization opportunities.
This would feed information directly into the Model Foundry and intelligent model router.
The important lesson: Lain should understand the hardware it's running on before deciding which models
and inference engines to use.
That's much more valuable than blindly selecting whichever runtime advertises the highest tokens per
second.

Lain Research — Stage 1: Existing Harnesses
1. The most interesting projects
These are the first projects I'd study closely. Each has something different to teach us.
1. Hermes Agent — The general-purpose agent
Hermes is particularly relevant to Lain's long-term vision.
Its architecture includes:
A broad tool registry.
Toolsets that can be enabled or disabled.
Persistent memory and session search.
Procedural memory through reusable skills.
Subagent delegation.
Browser automation and terminal execution.
MCP and plugin integrations.
Its interesting idea is that an agent can turn experience into reusable skills rather than repeatedly solving
the same problem from scratch. 
What Lain should learn: the relationship between memory, skills, and learning from experience.
2. OpenClaw — The extensible AI operating environment
OpenClaw has a particularly interesting architecture.
It separates:
Tools — actions the model can execute.
GitHub +1
Take your curiosity further
Scan to get the ChatGPT app
https://chatgpt.com/g/g-p-6aecffda1c588191b6f066b657f38c50-lain/c/6aecf929-3068-83ea-9e72-66b7e13e53f5 50/352

===== PAGE 51 =====
51 / 352
Skills — instructions for performing repeatable tasks.
Plugins — extensions that introduce new capabilities.
Agents — independent responsibilities and sessions.
Gateway — central coordination and routing.
One important detail: its gateway acts as the central source of truth for sessions, routing, and channel
connections. Its tool system also filters available tools according to permissions and execution policies.
What Lain should learn: a modular capability system, with a clear distinction between tools, skills, plugins,
and agents.
3. OpenHands — The execution environment
OpenHands is worth studying for its separation of responsibilities.
Its architecture distinguishes the interface from the agent server and execution environment. The frontend
doesn't directly execute agent actions or provide the sandbox. Those responsibilities belong to separate
backend components. 
What Lain should learn: don't let the UI, agent reasoning, and command execution become one giant
interconnected system.
4. Pi Coding Agent — Extensibility and customization
Pi has an interesting extension system.
Extensions can:
Register custom tools.
Intercept lifecycle events.
Modify context and compaction.
Register commands.
Add custom TUI components.
Persist extension state across sessions.
There's an important security tradeoff: extensions run inside the Pi process with the same operating-system
permissions. That makes extensions powerful, but also means untrusted extensions can be dangerous.

What Lain should learn: an extensible interface and lifecycle system, but with stronger isolation and

5. Mini-SWE-Agent — Simplicity
This is one of the most important projects to study, despite being much smaller than the others.
Its core agent is roughly 100 lines of Python. It uses:
Bash as its primary tool.
Independent subprocess executions.
A straightforward linear interaction history.
Replaceable model and environment components.
Its philosophy is that a capable model doesn't necessarily need an enormous agent framework around it.
What Lain should learn: keep the fundamental agent loop understandable. Complexity should exist
because it solves a real problem, not because it looks impressive.

===== PAGE 53 =====

A particularly interesting Codex implementation detail is that sandbox authority is passed through MCP
metadata. Its documentation explicitly warns that missing authority metadata does not authorize an
unsandboxed fallback. That's very close to the fail-closed philosophy we want for Lain. 
Claude Code's lifecycle hooks are another useful pattern: deterministic actions can run before or after tool
execution, after compaction, or when a session starts. This lets the harness enforce certain behaviors
without relying entirely on the model remembering instructions. 
3. My first architectural conclusions
These are preliminary recommendations, not final decisions.
A. Lain needs a small, dependable core
I don't think we should build a giant autonomous multi-agent framework immediately.
The initial runtime should have:
1. A session manager.
2. A model/provider interface.
3. An agent execution loop.
4. A tool registry.
5. A permission and policy engine.
6. An execution environment.
7. Persistent session history.
8. Logging and recovery.
Everything else should connect through well-defined interfaces.
B. Tools, skills, plugins, and agents must be separate concepts
I'd establish these definitions:
GitHub
Claude Help Ce…
Component Responsibility
Tool Performs an individual action
Skill Teaches the agent how to perform a task
Take your curiosity further
Scan to get the ChatGPT app
https://chatgpt.com/g/g-p-6aecffda1c588191b6f066b657f38c50-lain/c/6aecf929-3068-83ea-9e72-66b7e13e53f5 53/352

===== PAGE 54 =====
54 / 352
This distinction is important. Otherwise, we'll eventually have five overlapping systems trying to manage the
same functionality.
C. Security must exist from the beginning
Not something we bolt on after Lain becomes autonomous.
The model should never be the authority deciding whether its own actions are permitted.
The runtime should independently enforce:
Filesystem permissions.
Command execution restrictions.
Network permissions.
Tool access.
Approval requirements.
Execution budgets.
Audit logging.
Codex and OpenHands offer particularly useful examples of separating execution authority from agent
reasoning. 
D. Don't overbuild the agent loop
Mini-SWE-Agent provides an important counterargument to the idea that Lain needs an extremely
sophisticated agent loop immediately.
Start with a transparent execution loop. Add planning, delegation, retries, collaboration, and advanced
orchestration as independently testable capabilities.
Component Responsibility
Plugin Adds or packages capabilities
Agent Reasons and performs delegated responsibilities
Runtime Executes and coordinates agents
Model provider Supplies model inference

5. What still needs investigating
This first pass has revealed some promising patterns, but there are important gaps:
OpenCode: its provider architecture, session model, and tool execution deserve a deeper source-
level inspection.
Hermes: we need to understand exactly how its memory consolidation and learning mechanisms
work.
OpenClaw: we need to investigate its gateway, context engine, and multi-agent isolation in more
depth.
Codex: we should examine its sandbox and execution architecture.
Aider: we should investigate its repository mapping and coding context strategies.
All projects: we need to distinguish features that genuinely work from features that merely exist in
documentation.
The multi-site research is still being processed, so I'm not treating this first pass as the completed research
report.
My biggest takeaway so far
Lain should combine the architectural discipline of Codex, the extensibility of Pi, the general-purpose
capabilities of Hermes, the modularity of OpenClaw, and the simplicity of Mini-SWE-Agent.
But we should borrow patterns, not entire implementations.
Next, I'll continue the source-level investigation and turn these observations into a proper comparison and
decision record.

Research update: Stage 1, deeper architectural findings

Agent loop

Gateway and integrations

===== PAGE 58 =====
58 / 352
Its session storage is also worth studying. Hermes uses SQLite with full-text search, session lineage, and
persistent message history. Its documentation describes a single canonical session writer, rather than
allowing every interface to independently modify the database. 
Lain takeaway: one central runtime and one authoritative session system, with multiple interfaces
connecting to them.
I would not copy Hermes wholesale. Its large tool registry and broad integration surface are more
complexity than Lain needs initially.
2. OpenClaw: gateway and multi-agent architecture
OpenClaw's documentation reveals a useful architectural distinction:
The gateway is the central authority for sessions, routing, and channel connections. Agents, interfaces, and
integrations communicate through it.
It also documents:
Separate agent workspaces.
Multi-agent routing.
Isolated sessions.
Skills and plugins.
Sandboxing.
Browser control.
Session management.
Memory and context engines.
Its documentation is unusually extensive, making it a valuable reference when we eventually design Lain's
broader operating environment. 
Lain takeaway: a central coordination layer can make multiple interfaces and agents manageable without
making them one enormous application.
3. OpenHands: execution should be independent
OpenHands documents a frontend/backend architecture with an event stream, storage, LLM providers, and
a separate runtime interface.
Its runtime can connect to Docker, local, or remote execution environments. The agent produces actions;
the execution environment performs them and returns observations.

This is important because it means the model doesn't need direct access to the machine running the

4. Codex: security is an execution policy, not a prompt
The Codex source code is especially interesting.
Its execution policy considers:
Approval requirements.
Filesystem sandbox restrictions.
Network permissions.
Dangerous command detection.
Workspace trust.
Whether an operation requests additional privileges.
The implementation explicitly handles situations where dangerous commands or missing sandbox
protection should require approval rather than silently proceeding. 
Lain takeaway: permission checks must happen outside the model's reasoning process.
I want Lain's security system to have a policy decision before every consequential tool execution:

===== PAGE 60 =====
60 / 352
The policy engine should not be able to silently downgrade to unrestricted execution when a sandbox fails.
5. OpenCode: specialized agents and context costs
OpenCode has primary agents and subagents with configurable models, instructions, and tool permissions.
Its built-in Plan agent, for example, can analyze and review without modifying the codebase.
It also supports fine-grained permission rules: allow, ask, or deny. 
One particularly relevant warning appears in its MCP documentation: MCP servers can add substantial tool
definitions to the model's context, and enabling too many can consume a significant number of tokens.
Lain takeaway: dynamic tool discovery and selective tool exposure are important. We shouldn't load every
available tool into every agent's context.
6. Pi: powerful extensions need isolation
Pi's extension system can register tools, intercept lifecycle events, modify compaction, add commands, and
customize the TUI. 
But its security documentation makes an important tradeoff explicit: extensions run with the same
operating-system permissions as the main process unless an external isolation boundary exists. 
Lain takeaway: Pi is a great reference for extensibility, but Lain's eventual Creator Engine should package
and test extensions in isolated environments before they become trusted.

3. Three things I particularly want to avoid
Complexity trap 1: Building an agent framework inside an agent framework
We shouldn't make Lain depend on several large orchestration frameworks just to get basic tool calling,
planning, and memory.
My preference: own the core runtime and integrate external systems through clearly defined interfaces.
Complexity trap 2: Giving every agent every tool
That increases context consumption, makes permissions harder to reason about, and gives agents
capabilities they don't need.
My preference: agents receive tools based on their role, task, and permission level.
Complexity trap 3: Treating memory as a giant vector database
Hermes demonstrates that ordinary structured storage and full-text search already provide useful session
persistence and recall. That doesn't mean vector search is useless; it means we should establish the actual
retrieval requirements before adding it.
My preference: start with structured memory and search, then benchmark whether semantic retrieval and
graph relationships justify their complexity.

Important distinction
A provider, endpoint, and model should be separate objects.
For example:
Provider: MyGateway
Endpoint: https://example.com/v1
Protocol: OpenAI-compatible Chat Completions
Model: deepseek-something
Credential: An independently stored API key
You should be able to register multiple endpoints under one provider, and multiple models under each
endpoint.
You should also be able to register two different providers that expose the exact same underlying model.
That flexibility matters when you're experimenting with different gateways and comparing their
performance.
Required custom-provider features
│   └── Custom adapters
│
└── Intelligent Router
    ├── Model selection
    ├── Task classification
    ├── Capability matching
    ├── Cost constraints
    └── Availability and rate limits
Feature Priority
Custom base URLs Essential
Custom API keys and authentication Essential
Custom headers Essential

===== PAGE 68 =====
68 / 352
Lain should also distinguish between API compatibility and actual capability.
Just because an endpoint accepts OpenAI-compatible requests doesn't mean it supports streaming, tool
calling, reasoning fields, image inputs, or every other feature.
Lain needs to test or explicitly record those capabilities rather than assume them.
OpenCode's documentation specifically distinguishes between Chat Completions and Responses API
implementations, which is a good example of why protocol compatibility needs to be modeled explicitly.

===== PAGE 69 =====
69 / 352
A few particularly important findings:
A. Keep the agent runtime independent of the provider implementation.
The agent shouldn't care whether its model is running locally, through a gateway, or through a
conventional API provider.
It should interact with a standardized model interface.
B. Don't expose every tool to every agent.
OpenCode's documentation makes clear that MCP tool definitions can consume significant context. Lain
should load tools dynamically based on the current agent and task. 
C. Security needs to exist outside the model.
Pi's security documentation highlights that extensions and tools can inherit the permissions of the process
running them. A prompt telling an agent not to do something is not a security boundary. 
D. Keep execution replaceable.
OpenHands separates its agent backend from execution runtimes, while mini-SWE-agent separates agent,
environment, and model implementations. This is a strong foundation for Lain's future
local/container/remote execution options. 
Project What Lain should learn from it
Hermes Agent Central orchestration, provider resolution, session persistence, plugin
architecture
OpenCode Custom providers, permissions, specialized agents, configurable tools
OpenHands Separate agent reasoning from execution environments
Pi Extensible lifecycle hooks, custom tools, context management
mini-SWE-agent Small, replaceable components and simple agent loops
Goose MCP extensions, reusable agents, isolated delegation

5. Where the research goes next
Stage 1 is now sufficiently developed to establish the main architectural patterns, although some source-
level comparisons remain incomplete.
The next major area should be Stage 2: Persistent Memory.
I want to investigate:
How Hermes handles persistent memory and session history.
How OpenClaw handles memory files and retrieval.
How Mem0, Letta, and other memory systems approach long-term memory.
Whether GraphRAG actually provides enough value to justify its complexity.
How to preserve memory across agents, projects, and model providers.
How to prevent memory corruption, duplicate facts, stale information, and contradictory decisions.
How to make memory token-efficient without losing important context.
One important design principle is already clear:
Lain's memory must belong to Lain, not to whichever model happens to be running it.
Switching from one provider to another should never mean losing the agent's accumulated knowledge.
That's especially important for your custom-provider setup.
You
go ahead with stage 2.

ChatGPT
LAIN — Stage 2: Persistent Memory Research
My main conclusion: Lain needs a memory system that behaves more like a continuously maintained
knowledge base than a chatbot with a really long conversation history.
It should remember what matters, know where that information came from, recognize when something
changes, retrieve relevant context without wasting tokens, and share knowledge between agents without
mixing up their responsibilities.
I researched the memory architectures of Hermes, OpenClaw, Mem0, Graphiti, LangGraph, and current
long-term memory benchmarks.
There are some genuinely useful design decisions here.
1. What existing memory systems get right
Hermes Agent — curated memory + searchable history
Hermes Agent uses two small persistent memory files:
MEMORY.md : environmental facts, conventions, and things the agent has learned.
USER.md : user preferences and expectations.
These are loaded into the system prompt at session startup.
Its full conversation history is stored separately in SQLite, with FTS5 full-text search. This allows the agent to
retrieve actual messages from previous conversations rather than relying entirely on summaries.
What Lain should take from this:
Keep important memory small and immediately accessible. Keep the full history somewhere searchable.
Don't put everything into the system prompt.
Hermes Agent +1

===== PAGE 74 =====
74 / 352
OpenClaw — multiple memory tiers
OpenClaw has a more interesting approach.
Its memory architecture separates:
It uses Markdown files as inspectable storage, with SQLite providing indexing and retrieval. Its search
system can combine keyword and semantic retrieval. 
What Lain should take from this:
Different kinds of knowledge need different storage and retrieval strategies.
A project decision shouldn't be treated like a conversation transcript. A scheduled intention shouldn't be
treated like a user preference.
Mem0 — extracting useful facts
Mem0 takes a different approach.
Instead of storing every conversation verbatim as memory, it extracts reusable facts.
For example:
Conversation:
Potential memory:
Tier Purpose
Curated memory Important durable facts and preferences
Episodic memory Daily notes and historical events
Prospective memory Standing intentions and future actions
Review memory Consolidation reports and memory maintenance
OpenClaw +1
I recently moved from Austin to Seattle.

===== PAGE 75 =====
75 / 352
The system can use semantic, keyword, entity, and temporal signals to retrieve relevant information. It also
supports scoping memories by user, agent, and run. 
One important distinction: Mem0's open-source implementation and hosted platform do not have identical
graph-memory capabilities. We shouldn't assume that every advertised feature is available in a free, self-
hosted installation. 
What Lain should take from this:
Extract useful information instead of endlessly accumulating transcripts.
But don't silently discard the original source.
Graphiti — temporal knowledge graphs
Graphiti is particularly interesting.
It represents knowledge through:
Entities.
Relationships.
Episodes.
Timestamps.
Historical changes.
Suppose Lain learns:
1. Project A uses Python.
2. Project A switches to Rust.
3. Project A later adopts a Rust/Python hybrid.
A conventional memory system might overwrite the original language preference.
Graphiti's temporal approach can preserve the history of changing facts and distinguish when something
was true from when the system learned it. Its retrieval combines semantic, keyword, and graph-based
approaches. 
What Lain should take from this:
User's current location is Seattle.

===== PAGE 82 =====
82 / 352
Search methods
1. Full-text search
Best for exact names, identifiers, file paths, error messages, and technical details.
2. Semantic search
Best for finding related information when the wording differs.
3. Entity search
Best for finding everything related to a particular project, model, provider, agent, or tool.
4. Graph traversal
Best for following relationships between connected facts.
5. Temporal search
Best for questions involving changes and historical state.
I would combine these methods progressively rather than run every search mechanism on every request.
OpenClaw's hybrid search and Graphiti's combination of semantic, keyword, and graph retrieval provide
useful references. 
                       |

                PERMISSION CHECK
                       |
                       v
              RELEVANCE / FRESHNESS
                       |
                       v
               CONTEXT ASSEMBLY
                       |
                       v
                  MAIN AGENT

6. Do we actually need GraphRAG?
My answer: eventually, possibly. Not initially.
There are two different problems here.
Conventional RAG
You have a collection of documents and want to retrieve relevant sections.
Example:
Vector and keyword search can handle this.
GraphRAG
You have a collection of connected entities and want to understand relationships across them.
Example:
This is where graph-based retrieval becomes more interesting.
Microsoft's GraphRAG is designed to extract structured knowledge from unstructured text, but its
documentation warns that indexing can be expensive. 
Graphiti is arguably a closer conceptual match for Lain's evolving project and agent knowledge because it
handles temporal relationships and incremental updates. 
Find the previous discussion about Lain's model router.
Which projects depend on the model router, what decisions affected it, and which agents have worked
on those components?

===== PAGE 85 =====
85 / 352
Each agent gets:
Its own session history.
Its own working context.
Its own temporary notes.
Its own specialist knowledge.
Permission-controlled access to shared project memory.
An agent can propose a shared memory update, but it shouldn't silently overwrite important project
decisions.
For example:
The developer discovers that a previous architecture decision is impractical.
It submits a proposed change, including evidence.
The memory system records the proposal, checks it against the existing decision, and either updates the
decision following the required approval or preserves the disagreement.
This prevents multiple agents from accidentally rewriting shared project history.
LangGraph's distinction between thread-scoped checkpoints and cross-thread long-term stores is another
useful reference here.

I'll call it Memory Dreaming for now.
It should periodically:
1. Review recent conversations and task histories.

3. Detect repeated facts and duplicate memories.
4. Find contradictions.
5. Identify outdated information.
6. Update project summaries.
7. Extract reusable procedures.
8. Identify unresolved decisions.
9. Produce a review report.
10. Propose changes to durable memory.
Crucially, it shouldn't automatically rewrite everything.
High-impact changes should be reviewable and reversible.
A possible schedule:
This should run with low resource usage. It shouldn't constantly wake up a large model to reorganize a few
notes.

Duplicate detection During writes

Full memory review Periodically

9. Storage: what should Lain actually use?
My initial recommendation is SQLite.
Not because it's the most advanced option, but because it provides a practical starting point:
Local storage.
No mandatory external service.
Low resource overhead.
Structured data.
Transactions.
Full-text search through FTS5.
A straightforward backup story.
Hermes uses SQLite and FTS5 for session persistence, while OpenClaw also uses SQLite for indexing its
memory system.

The database should be the canonical source of truth for structured memory.

Embeddings
I would make embeddings optional and provider-independent.
This connects directly to the custom-provider architecture we discussed earlier.
Lain should support:
Local embedding models.
Configured cloud embedding endpoints.
OpenAI-compatible embedding endpoints.
Different embedding models for different indexes.
Keyword-only retrieval when no embedding model is configured.
If an embedding endpoint stops working, memory shouldn't stop working.
10. Memory evaluation: how do we know it's
actually good?
This is one of the most important discoveries from this research.
We shouldn't evaluate memory by asking whether the agent seems to remember things.
There are actual benchmarks.

===== PAGE 89 =====
89 / 352
LongMemEval
The 2025 ICLR LongMemEval benchmark evaluates:
Information extraction.
Multi-session reasoning.
Temporal reasoning.
Knowledge updates.
Abstention.
It contains 500 questions over long conversation histories. Its authors found that long-term memory
remains challenging even for capable assistants. 
Newer conversational evaluation
A 2026 EMNLP Findings paper introduced LOCOMO-CONV, which evaluates memory in more natural
conversational situations, including implicit and multi-part requests. It highlights that a memory system can
perform well on direct fact-retrieval questions but still fail to use remembered information naturally in
conversation. 
Lain's own benchmark suite
I want Lain to eventually have a memory benchmark with tests like:
ICLR Proceedings
ACL Anthology
Test Example
Basic recall What language does Project A use?
Historical recall What language did it use before?
Preference recall What are the user's cost constraints?
Multi-session recall What did we decide last week?
Conflict handling Two sources disagree. What happens?
Update handling A previous decision changes.
Scope isolation Can Agent A access Agent B's private notes?

===== PAGE 93 =====
93 / 352
12. One architectural decision I especially want to
preserve
Lain must never confuse remembering something with believing something.
A memory entry is evidence that information was recorded. It isn't proof that the information is correct or
still current.
This means:
Every important fact has a source.
Important facts can expire or become outdated.
Conflicting facts can coexist historically.
Corrections don't erase the historical record.
Agents can express uncertainty.
Retrieval respects project and agent permissions.
External content is treated as untrusted data, not privileged instructions.
This is particularly important once Lain can autonomously research, modify projects, and create its own
tools.
13. What's next?
I'd consider Stage 2's conceptual research complete enough to establish the memory architecture.
There are still implementation-level questions to benchmark, particularly SQLite versus alternative storage,
embedding models, and Graphiti integration. Those should be answered experimentally rather than by
choosing the most impressive-looking framework.
The next stage I'd recommend is:
Stage 3 — Model Intelligence & Routing
This is where we connect the memory architecture to your custom-provider requirement.

1. The central design principle
Lain should be model-provider agnostic.
It shouldn't be built around DeepSeek, OpenAI, Anthropic, OpenRouter, or any particular provider.
Instead, it should have its own internal model interface.
This means the agent runtime never needs to know how a particular provider works.
It asks Lain's model interface for a model that satisfies its requirements.
The interface handles the rest.

2. Custom providers and endpoints
I want to expand on what we discussed previously.
There are three different things Lain needs to manage independently.
Provider
The service supplying the inference.
Examples:
DeepSeek
OpenRouter
Groq
An independent inference service
Your own local server
Endpoint
The actual API address and configuration.
Examples:
A provider's official API.
An OpenAI-compatible endpoint.
A custom gateway.
A local inference server.
A proxy with its own authentication and headers.
Model
The actual model exposed by that endpoint.
Examples:
A particular DeepSeek model.
A GLM model.
A locally hosted Qwen model.
A model with a provider-specific alias.

One provider can expose multiple endpoints, and one endpoint can expose multiple models.
A model can also be available through multiple endpoints.
This is important because the same underlying model can behave differently depending on the inference

Why maintain our own intelligence database?
Provider metadata isn't always complete or accurate.
A model might claim to support tool calling but return malformed tool arguments in practice.
Another might have a huge context window but become unreliable when approaching its advertised
maximum.
A third might be excellent at coding but mediocre at structured research.
Lain should record actual results.

also explains custom pricing for self-hosted deployments.

5. Intelligent model routing
This is probably the most ambitious component of Stage 3.

It should select the best available model for the actual task.

A model must support the required input modality.
Its context window must accommodate the request.
Its endpoint must be available.
It must satisfy the user's budget policy.
It must support required tools if tool calling is necessary.
If a model fails one of those checks, it should be excluded.
Only after that should Lain score the remaining candidates.
Example scoring system
An illustrative starting formula:
These weights should be configurable by task type.
For a coding task, code quality and tool-call reliability might receive more weight.
For summarization, speed and cost efficiency might matter more.
Cost should primarily be a hard constraint, not something a model can compensate for by having a high
quality score.
That prevents an expensive model from winning simply because its other scores are excellent.
Metric Weight
Task-specific quality 35%
Reliability 20%
Speed 15%
Tool-call performance 15%
Context suitability 10%
Resource efficiency 5%

6. Your $0-only routing requirements
I want to preserve the strict rules from your earlier DSH work.
Lain should support explicit budget policies:
For your default configuration, I'd make Free Only the active policy.
The important distinction: free versus unknown
These are not the same thing.
This is particularly important because LiteLLM's cost map documentation explains that a missing model
price can result in a recorded cost of zero. That is a bookkeeping behavior, not proof that an API call was
free. 
Lain must never interpret missing pricing data as free.
Policy Behavior
Free only Only verified $0 options
Budget limited Enforce a configured maximum
Local only Only local inference
Privacy restricted Only approved endpoints
Manual User selects the model
Unrestricted Use configured eligible providers
VERIFIED FREE      -> Eligible
VERIFIED PAID      -> Rejected
LIMITED FREE       -> Eligible only under matching restrictions
UNKNOWN PRICE      -> Rejected in Free Only mode
UNKNOWN TIER       -> Rejected in Free Only mode

Pricing source.

Free-tier eligibility.

Unknown means blocked until verified.
7. Fallbacks, retries, and reliability
I want Lain to be much more intelligent about failures than simply switching to another model.
Consider these scenarios:
Failure Correct behavior
Temporary network error Retry within limits
Rate limit Respect retry-after and try an eligible endpoint
Provider outage Consider an eligible alternative
Invalid API key Mark endpoint unhealthy; don't loop retries
Unsupported capability Exclude model
Context too large Reassess context or choose suitable model

Scan to get the ChatGPT app
https://chatgpt.com/g/g-p-6aecffda1c588191b6f066b657f38c50-lain/c/6aecf929-3068-83ea-9e72-66b7e13e53f5 107/352

===== PAGE 108 =====
108 / 352
One crucial rule:
A fallback must never silently weaken the user's policy.
For example, if your selected model is unavailable, Lain must not quietly use a paid model.
It should either find another eligible model or explain why the task cannot continue.
LiteLLM's router separates retries within a model group from fallbacks to another model group. That's a
useful distinction to adopt, although Lain should implement stricter eligibility checks around every
transition. 
Request tracking
Every inference request should generate a record:
Failure Correct behavior
Unknown pricing Block under Free Only
Paid endpoint fallback Never under Free Only

8. Automatic model discovery
I want Lain to eventually be able to discover models from configured providers.
But automatic discovery must be separate from automatic trust.
For example:
1. Lain connects to a configured endpoint.
2. It retrieves available model IDs if supported.
3. It compares them against its existing registry.
4. It creates candidate model entries.
5. It retrieves metadata where available.
6. It tests supported capabilities.
7. It records pricing and provenance.
8. It presents newly discovered models for approval or applies a configured policy.
It should never automatically assume that a discovered model is free, safe, reliable, or capable of tool
calling.
This also creates a foundation for your future Research Lab.
9. Local models and hardware-aware routing
This connects directly to your hardware constraints.
Lain should treat local inference as a first-class provider category.
It should support configurable local endpoints rather than tightly coupling itself to Ollama or any single
inference engine.
Potential integrations include:
Ollama.
llama.cpp.

My biggest recommendation
I would avoid building Lain on top of a giant provider framework that dictates how the entire application
works.
LiteLLM is worth studying for provider coverage, cost tracking, and routing patterns, but Lain should own its
internal model interface and policy engine.
That way, we can use adapters or libraries where they make sense without making the entire system
dependent on one framework.

===== PAGE 119 =====

Prepaid-aware: track provider balances when the provider exposes them.
Free-first: prefer free models when quality and reliability are sufficient.
Quality-first: permit a more capable paid model when the task warrants it and budget allows.
Hard stop: never exceed the configured spending limit.
Fail-closed: unknown pricing or billing behavior cannot silently become an approved expense.
A $40 budget should be treated as a finite resource, not an invitation for agents to spend freely. Any actual
purchase should be reviewed with a parent or guardian.
Now, onto Stage 4.
LAIN — Stage 4: Agents & Orchestration
This is where Lain starts becoming more than a chatbot with tools.
I want it to become an environment where one main agent can coordinate specialists, create temporary
workers, run parallel projects, evaluate results, and manage complicated workflows.
But there's a problem with many multi-agent systems:
They make it incredibly easy to create agents and surprisingly difficult to control what those agents are
actually doing.
We need to get the architecture right.
1. Research: What existing agent systems teach us
I looked at several architectures, particularly Hermes, OpenCode, OpenHands, LangGraph, and AutoGen.
Hermes Agent
Hermes Agent has a particularly interesting delegation system.
Its delegate_task  mechanism creates independent child agents with:
Separate conversations.
Separate terminal sessions.
Independent context.
Take your curiosity further
Scan to get the ChatGPT app
https://chatgpt.com/g/g-p-6aecffda1c588191b6f066b657f38c50-lain/c/6aecf929-3068-83ea-9e72-66b7e13e53f5 119/352

===== PAGE 120 =====
120 / 352
Configurable model/provider overrides.
Optional structured output requirements.
Parallel execution.
Only the final result is returned to the parent, rather than every intermediate tool call. This helps prevent
child-agent activity from overwhelming the main context. 
One especially interesting feature is that the parent can use a more capable model while delegating simpler
tasks to a cheaper model.
What Lain should learn: Different agents should be allowed to use different models.
This fits your DeepSeek-centered setup extremely well.
OpenCode
OpenCode distinguishes between primary agents and subagents.
Its built-in examples include:
Build: implementation.
Plan: restricted planning.
General: multi-step delegated work.
Explore: read-only codebase exploration.
It also provides agent-specific tool permissions and controls over which agents can delegate to other
agents. 
What Lain should learn: An agent's identity should determine its capabilities and permissions, not just its
personality prompt.
OpenHands
OpenHands separates the agent's reasoning from the environment where its actions execute.
Its architecture uses an event stream, storage, runtime interface, and model providers. The runtime can
support different execution environments. 
What Lain should learn: Agents should not directly control the underlying operating system. They should
request actions through a controlled execution layer.
Hermes Agent +1
OpenCode +1
GitHub
Take your curiosity further
Scan to get the ChatGPT app
https://chatgpt.com/g/g-p-6aecffda1c588191b6f066b657f38c50-lain/c/6aecf929-3068-83ea-9e72-66b7e13e53f5 120/352

===== PAGE 121 =====
121 / 352
LangGraph
LangGraph is particularly interesting for complex workflows.
It supports:
Stateful workflows.
Multiple agents.
Conditional transitions.
Parallel execution.
Checkpointing.
Interruptions and resumption.
Its graph execution model is useful because tasks can be represented as nodes with explicit dependencies
rather than an uncontrolled conversation between agents. 
What Lain should learn: Complex agent workflows should have explicit structure and recoverable state.
AutoGen
Microsoft's AutoGen demonstrated several collaboration patterns:
Round-robin teams.
Dynamically selected speakers.
Agent handoffs.
Orchestrated teams.
Its documentation also warns that multi-agent teams require more scaffolding and aren't necessarily better
than a properly configured single agent. Importantly, the AutoGen repository is now archived, so I would
treat it as a source of architectural lessons rather than a foundation to adopt. 
What Lain should learn: Collaboration patterns should be selected based on the task, not applied
everywhere.

There are four important agent types.
A. Main Agent
This is the primary agent you interact with.
It should:
Understand your goals.
Maintain the overall context.
Plan complicated work.
Select specialists.
Delegate tasks.
Coordinate execution.
Review results.
Report progress.
Maintain continuity.
It should not personally perform every operation.
Think of it as the central operator of Lain.

B. Permanent Specialists
These are persistent agent definitions with specific responsibilities.
Examples:
They have stable instructions, preferred models, tools, and memory scopes.

Its identity and configuration persist. Its actual execution can start when needed.
That saves resources.

Context isolation
One of the most important lessons from Hermes is that subagents don't automatically inherit the parent's
entire conversation history.
The parent must provide the context they need. 
I would preserve this.
Instead of copying a massive conversation into every child, Lain should generate a context package.
The result is smaller, more relevant, and easier to audit.

Verification should be independent
I don't want the developer agent to simply claim that its implementation works.
A separate tester or verification process should inspect the result.
For example:
Developer implements a feature.
Tester runs relevant tests.
Reviewer inspects the changes.
Main agent checks whether the requested objective was actually achieved.
This doesn't mean every tiny task needs three agents.
For a simple task, one agent and automated tests may be sufficient.
The orchestrator should scale verification effort according to the task's complexity and consequences.
10. Agent recovery and interruption
Long-running tasks will fail.
Models will disconnect. Providers will rate-limit requests. Agents will get stuck. Tools will time out.
Lain needs to treat these as normal operational events.
I recommend these task states:
           PASS                 FAIL
             |                   |
             v                   v
          COMPLETE           RECOVERY
                                 |
                           REASSESS TASK
                                 |
                           RETRY / REPLAN
Take your curiosity further
Scan to get the ChatGPT app
https://chatgpt.com/g/g-p-6aecffda1c588191b6f066b657f38c50-lain/c/6aecf929-3068-83ea-9e72-66b7e13e53f5 135/352

===== PAGE 136 =====
136 / 352
Recovery behavior
If an agent crashes:
1. Preserve its execution history.
2. Preserve completed artifacts.
3. Record the last checkpoint.
4. Determine whether the operation is safe to resume.
5. Restart from a recoverable point.
6. Avoid repeating completed external side effects.
LangGraph's checkpointing documentation is useful here: workflows can resume from saved state, but
operations must be designed to tolerate retries. 
Lain should use idempotency keys for operations that might otherwise be repeated.
And importantly, a user-requested cancellation should propagate to child agents and their descendants.
State Meaning
Created Task registered
Pending Waiting to execute
Scheduled Resources assigned
Running Agent working
Waiting Awaiting dependency or approval
Interrupted Paused by user or system
Failed Unrecoverable failure
Completed Verified completion
Cancelled Explicitly stopped

15. Decisions I'd preserve
My biggest architectural recommendation
Build Lain around tasks and events, not around agents talking to each other endlessly.

Main agent One persistent central operator

Temporary agents Dynamically generated and disposable

Parallelism Resource-aware

Agents are workers. Tasks are the actual units of work. Events record what happened. The orchestrator
decides what happens next.
That gives us a system that can scale from one agent to dozens without fundamentally changing how it
operates.

Creator Engine
This is where we research how Lain should extend its own capabilities.
I want to cover:
1. Native tool architecture.

5. Your custom Creator Mode.

7. Agent-specific tool access.
8. Tool testing and validation.
9. Sandboxing and permission boundaries.
10. Self-improvement without uncontrolled modifications.
And I want to preserve one distinction: a skill teaches an agent how to do something; a tool gives it the
ability to do something; a plugin packages new functionality into Lain.
Those should be separate concepts, even if they integrate closely.

I researched the current MCP specification, official documentation for OpenClaw, Hermes, and OpenCode,
the Agent Skills standard, and OWASP's MCP security guidance.
One important update: the MCP specification released on July 28, 2026, so some older MCP tutorials and
architectural recommendations are already outdated. I'll account for that below. 
1. First: Understand the four capability types
I think Lain needs four distinct concepts:
These shouldn't be interchangeable.
Tools: Give Lain the ability to act
A tool is a typed function exposed to an agent.
Examples:
read_file
search_files
execute_command
edit_file
browser_navigate
browser_click

or data source

functionality to Lain

search_web

Tools should have explicit schemas, descriptions, permission requirements, execution limits, and result

Skills: Teach Lain how to work
A skill doesn't necessarily introduce a new capability.
For example, Lain might already have terminal, file, Git, and testing tools.
A Rust debugging skill teaches it how to combine those tools into a repeatable debugging process.
I strongly recommend adopting the open Agent Skills format instead of inventing an entirely proprietary
format.
A standard skill directory looks like:
The standard supports metadata, instructions, and optional supporting resources. 
Important: Use progressive disclosure.
1. Lain initially knows a skill's name and description.
2. It loads the full instructions when relevant.
3. It retrieves supporting documents only when needed.
4. It executes bundled scripts only when necessary.
rust-debugging/
├── SKILL.md
├── references/
│   ├── common-errors.md
│   └── debugging-guide.md
├── scripts/
│   └── diagnostics.py
└── assets/
GitHub +1

This is already used by Hermes and helps avoid bloating every agent's context with hundreds of irrelevant

MCP: Connect to the outside world
Model Context Protocol is a standardized way for applications to expose capabilities and information to AI
systems.
Its main server capabilities include:
Tools: Actions an agent can invoke.
Resources: Data that an agent can read.
Prompts: Reusable prompt templates.
For example, a GitHub MCP server might expose tools to inspect repositories and issues, resources
containing repository information, and prompts for common workflows. 
My recommendation: Lain should implement MCP as an integration protocol, not make MCP the
foundation of its entire internal tool system.
Why?
Because Lain needs native capabilities that don't depend on an external protocol. MCP should make
external integrations easier, not dictate how every internal action works.
Plugins: Package complete capabilities
A plugin can combine:
Tools.
Skills.
MCP connections.
Configuration.
Credentials.
Hooks.
Background services.
UI components.
Agent definitions.
Other plugin-specific resources.
Hermes Agent +1
MCP TypeScript … +1

2. Lain's Native Tool Architecture
I recommend building a central Capability Registry.
Every available capability should be registered in one place, regardless of whether it comes from Lain itself,
an MCP server, or a plugin.
Proposed architecture:
OpenClaw +1
A skill teaches Lain how to do something. A tool lets it do something. An MCP integration connects it to
something. A plugin packages new functionality into something installable.

Tool discovery
One problem with larger agent systems is exposing every available tool to every model request.

  ],

    "runtime": "local",
    "timeout_seconds": 15
  }
}

        ↓

  cargo_check
  cargo_test
  rust_analyzer_diagnostics
  dependency_audit
  filesystem_search

  Agent permissions
  Task requirements
  Availability
  Compatibility

===== PAGE 153 =====
153 / 352
The July 2026 MCP update matters
The current specification introduces a stateless protocol core.
Notable changes include:
Removal of the old initialization/session handshake in the new protocol era.
A new server/discover  method for capability discovery.
Header-based routing for Streamable HTTP.
Cache hints for tool, resource, and prompt listings.
Authorization hardening.
A formal extension framework.
Changes to long-running task handling.
The old HTTP+SSE transport is deprecated in favor of Streamable HTTP. Roots, Sampling, and Logging are
also deprecated for new implementations. 
My recommendation:
Component Responsibility
Server Registry Tracks configured MCP servers
Connection Manager Starts, stops, and monitors connections
Protocol Adapter Handles MCP versions and transports
Discovery Engine Retrieves available tools, resources, and prompts
Schema Registry Stores and validates capability definitions
Credential Manager Stores credentials securely
Permission Mapper Maps external capabilities to Lain permissions
Health Monitor Detects disconnected or malfunctioning servers
Audit Logger Records calls and results
Model Context … +2
Take your curiosity further
Scan to get the ChatGPT app
https://chatgpt.com/g/g-p-6aecffda1c588191b6f066b657f38c50-lain/c/6aecf929-3068-83ea-9e72-66b7e13e53f5 153/352

===== PAGE 154 =====
154 / 352
Don't manually implement MCP from scratch.
Use an official SDK and design Lain's adapter around protocol-version compatibility.
Support existing servers using older protocol versions while making the new version the preferred path.
This prevents Lain from becoming incompatible with a large portion of the existing MCP ecosystem.
MCP long-running tasks
Some MCP operations may take minutes rather than seconds.
The newer Tasks extension provides a way to track these operations through task handles, polling, updates,
and cancellation. 
This could be useful for:
Long-running research.
Repository analysis.
Dataset processing.
External builds.
Large integration workflows.
However, Lain should still maintain its own Task Manager. MCP tasks should be represented as external
work within Lain's task system, not replace it.

The distinction between installed and enabled is important.
A plugin can exist on disk without having permission to execute.
OpenClaw's documentation demonstrates useful ideas here: pinned versions, installation policies, allowlists,
runtime inspection, and explicit verification that the running system actually loaded the expected plugin.
Plugin isolation
I would avoid loading arbitrary third-party plugin code directly into Lain's main process.
Instead:
Plugins should have:
Explicit permissions.
Restricted filesystem access.
Network restrictions.
Dependency isolation.
Resource limits.
Version pinning.
INSTALLED
    ↓
ENABLED
    ↓
MONITORED
    ↓
UPDATED / DISABLED / REMOVED
OpenClaw +1
Plugin trust Execution
Built-in, reviewed Native runtime where appropriate
Verified third-party Restricted plugin runtime
Unknown source Quarantined inspection
Untrusted or rejected Cannot execute

Step 2: Inspect existing capabilities
Before creating anything, Lain should check whether it already has the required functionality.
This prevents duplicated tools and unnecessary dependencies.
Possible outcomes:
1. Existing tools already cover everything: create a skill.
2. One new action is required: create a native tool.
3. An external service needs connecting: create an MCP integration.
4. Multiple capabilities need packaging: create a plugin.
This distinction is important.
Not everything should become a plugin.
Step 3: Design before implementation
Generate a structured design manifest describing:
Plugin purpose.
Required capabilities.
Proposed tools.
Required skills.
External services.
Dependencies.
Permissions.
Configuration.
Security boundaries.
Tests.
Expected outputs.
The architecture should be reviewed before code generation.

The main Creator Engine coordinates everything.
Step 5: Install and verify
A successful build doesn't automatically mean a plugin is safe.
Creator Mode should prove:
The manifest is valid.
Dependencies resolve.
Tools register correctly.
Permission boundaries work.
Tests pass.
Invalid inputs are rejected.
Expected functionality works.
Failure conditions are handled.
The runtime actually loads the extension.
The result should include a report containing test results, known limitations, permissions, dependencies,
and provenance.
No automatic installation or enabling without approval.
Agent Responsibility
Architect Design the extension
Researcher Research APIs and existing implementations
Developer Implement it
Tester Create and execute tests
Security Review permissions and vulnerabilities
Documentation Generate documentation

7. Plugin Intelligence & Discovery

The discovery system should separate availability from trust.
Finding a plugin does not mean it's safe. Popularity does not establish security. A successful security scan
does not prove that the plugin works correctly.

8. Security: The part I would not compromise
The research here surfaced some important problems.
OWASP identifies several MCP-specific threats:
Tool poisoning.
Tool description manipulation.
Plugin or server updates that change behavior.
Cross-server tool shadowing.
Excessive permissions.
Credential exposure.
Prompt injection through tool results.
Untrusted server execution. 
This is why I would build a dedicated security layer rather than relying on individual tools to behave
correctly.
A. Permissions must be enforced outside the model
A model saying "I am allowed to do this" means nothing.
The policy engine must independently check every execution request.
OWASP Cheat S… +1
Take your curiosity further
Scan to get the ChatGPT app
https://chatgpt.com/g/g-p-6aecffda1c588191b6f066b657f38c50-lain/c/6aecf929-3068-83ea-9e72-66b7e13e53f5 164/352

===== PAGE 165 =====
165 / 352
B. Tool schemas must be reviewed and pinned
OWASP recommends reviewing complete tool definitions and detecting changes to schemas and
annotations.
This matters because an external server could change what a previously approved tool does.
Lain should store a cryptographic hash of each reviewed definition and require re-review when it changes.
C. Tool results are untrusted
A web page, document, MCP server, or plugin can return malicious instructions.
Lain should treat external tool results as data, not instructions that override its operating rules.
D. Credentials must be isolated
Each integration should have its own credential scope.
For example, a read-only GitHub integration should not automatically receive permissions to modify
repositories.
OWASP explicitly recommends least privilege, scoped credentials, isolated servers, and approval for
sensitive actions. 
E. Installation needs its own security gate
The Creator Engine should not be able to bypass the plugin security system.
Even if Lain generates the plugin itself, the same validation and approval process should apply.

Graph
This is where your future Obsidian-on-crack idea becomes especially interesting.

10. Recommended implementation order
I would resist trying to implement all of this at once.
Here's the order I'd use:
Priority Component Why
1 Native Tool Registry Foundation for every capability
2 Tool Execution Broker Centralized execution and policy
3 Permission Integration Prevent unauthorized actions
4 Tool Discovery Expose only relevant capabilities
5 Skills Engine Reusable procedural knowledge
6 MCP Client External integrations
7 MCP Manager Lifecycle and connection management
8 Plugin Manifest & Registry Package capabilities
9 Plugin Runtime Controlled extension execution
10 Plugin Security Pipeline Validate extensions
11 Creator Engine Generate tools, skills, and plugins
12 Plugin Intelligence Improve and maintain capabilities
13 Public Marketplace Discover and share extensions

===== PAGE 169 =====
169 / 352
Three particularly important findings
1. Lain should own its capability system.
MCP, Agent Skills, and external plugins should integrate into Lain's architecture rather than becoming its
architecture.
2. Creator Mode should create the smallest useful extension.
If a skill solves the problem, don't generate a plugin. If a single native tool solves it, don't generate an entire
package.
3. Security and execution must be separate from agent reasoning.
The model can propose an action, but Lain determines whether that action is permitted.

Plugins Manifest-driven packages
Plugin execution Isolated runtime
Tool execution Central execution broker
Permissions Independently enforced policy engine

Plugin installation Explicit approval
Plugin updates Pinned versions and rollback
Creator Mode Full research → design → build → test → review pipeline
Registry Local-first, external sources optional
Graph Track capabilities, dependencies, provenance, and execution history

Research status: Complete.
I also investigated Claude Code's new Mods system, including Anthropic's announcement, its published
implementation, built-in mods, and community examples.
And I agree with you: this is one of the more interesting developments in agent architecture. It's not just
another plugin system. It introduces a much deeper way to customize an AI application's actual behavior.
I think Lain should borrow this idea and take it further.
I'll cover that first, because it changes how I think we should structure Lain's extension architecture.
Part 1 — Claude Code Mods: Why they're
genuinely interesting
1. What Anthropic actually introduced
On October 1, 2026, Anthropic introduced Mods for Claude Code.
A mod is a TypeScript extension that can intercept and modify Claude Code's internal events.
Unlike traditional hooks, mods can:

Rewrite prompts before they reach the model.

Official announcement: Claude Code Mods
2. The architecture is the genius part
Here's the basic idea.
Every time Claude Code performs an operation, it emits an event.
A mod registers functions that intercept those events.

Before an operation.
After an operation.

The extension isn't merely reacting to an event.
It's participating in the application's execution pipeline.
That's the important distinction.
Part 2 — What Claude Mods can actually
accomplish
Anthropic has published built-in and example mods.
Some are particularly interesting.
1. The built-in /diff  feature
Claude's /diff  interface is now implemented as a mod.
This is a major architectural decision.
Instead of every feature being permanently embedded in the application's core, some built-in functionality
can be implemented through the same extension mechanism available to users.
It can be disabled or replaced. 
This suggests a different philosophy:
The core application should provide the engine. Extensions should provide much of the behavior.
Before tool execution:
    Inspect requested action
    Modify arguments
    Ask another component for context
    Display a custom confirmation interface
    Continue with modified arguments
    Inspect the result
    Modify the result
    Return it
Claude +1

===== PAGE 175 =====
175 / 352
2. Token Weather
One of Anthropic's example mods adds a live context-window indicator above the prompt.
It could show how much context is available and give the user a visual indication of context pressure.
3. Replay Theater
Another example lets you step through the file edits Claude made during its previous turn.
Think of it as a timeline of an agent's changes.
4. Blast Radius
This is particularly clever.
The mod intercepts risky commands and presents a review interface before execution.
The user can inspect the proposed operation and choose whether to proceed.
The example includes operations such as destructive filesystem commands, hard resets, and force pushes.
These examples demonstrate that mods can become actual product features, not just small customizations.

I would introduce a new architectural concept:
Lain Mod Engine
This would be a separate extension layer sitting between Lain's core systems and the capabilities exposed
to agents.

B. Mods should be able to replace built-in features
This is the most important design decision.
Suppose Lain has a built-in model router.
Someone develops a better routing algorithm.
Instead of requiring a fork of Lain, they could create a mod that replaces the routing decision handler.
Similarly:
Replace the default memory retrieval strategy.
Replace the task scheduler.
Add a custom agent-selection policy.
Replace the default terminal interface.

178 / 352
Introduce an alternative context-compaction strategy.
Add custom project dashboards.
This makes Lain much more adaptable.
However, I would distinguish replaceable behavior from non-replaceable safety infrastructure.
The permission enforcement engine, audit integrity, and spending limits must not be replaceable by
ordinary mods.
A mod may propose a permission decision or display an approval interface. The independent policy engine
must retain final authority.
C. Mods should communicate with one another
Anthropic's implementation has an interesting approach to shared extension contracts.
Its published source shows mods declaring typed interfaces for shared engine capabilities. Other mods can
consume those interfaces instead of duplicating their definitions. 
I'd adopt this concept.
For example:
The mods don't need to know each other's internal implementation.
They only need to agree on a typed contract.

D. Creator Mode should generate mods
This is where Stage 5 and Stage 6 connect.
Imagine telling Lain:
Creator Mode should:
1. Inspect Lain's available event APIs.
2. Determine which events it needs.
3. Generate a mod manifest.
4. Implement the TypeScript.
5. Create the interface.
6. Generate tests.
7. Run it in an isolated development environment.
8. Verify the mod's behavior.
9. Package it.
10. Ask permission to install and enable it.
Then it hot-reloads the extension without restarting the entire application.
That's the experience I'd aim for.
Part 4 — The important difference: Lain Mods
must be safer
There's one thing I would not copy from Anthropic.
Anthropic explicitly states that Claude Code mods run with the same machine access as Claude Code itself.
They are not sandboxed. 
That's a significant security tradeoff.
Create a mod that shows me everything my agents are doing, including their active tasks, model usage,
context consumption, and resource usage.

For Lain, I recommend three trust levels.
The runtime should enforce:
Filesystem permissions.
Network permissions.
API access.
Resource limits.
Execution timeouts.
Event subscription permissions.
Mod dependency validation.
Version compatibility.
Rollback.
I'd also make mod execution deterministic where possible.
If five mods intercept the same event, Lain needs a predictable execution order.
Suggested ordering:
1. System safety middleware.
2. Organization policy middleware.
3. User-configured mods.
4. Project-specific mods.
5. Agent-specific mods.
6. Original operation.
But safety middleware must remain outside the authority of those extensions.
One additional idea: Mod Inspector
Every mod should have an inspector panel.
Trust level Capabilities
Trusted Full approved mod API access
Restricted Explicitly granted capabilities through an isolated runtime
Untrusted Inspection and testing only; no access to live sessions

182 / 352
Coordinating work across local and remote environments.
The important thing is that these activities should not all use the same execution mechanism.
1. Four levels of computer interaction
I recommend this hierarchy:
This is important because screenshot-based automation should not be the default.
It is often slower and less reliable than interacting with the underlying interface directly.
Level 1 — Native APIs
This is the preferred option.
Examples:
Git operations through Git.
Project information through filesystem APIs.
GitHub through its API.
Browser data through browser APIs.
Build systems through their CLI.
Application data through official integrations.
If Lain can accomplish something through a reliable API, it shouldn't need to simulate mouse clicks.
Level 2 — Browser automation
For websites, I'd use Playwright as an initial candidate.
Level Method Best use
1 Native APIs and integrations Structured operations
2 Browser/DOM automation Websites and web applications
3 Accessibility/UI automation Desktop applications
4 Vision-based computer use Interfaces without reliable structured access

Microsoft provides Windows UI Automation, an accessibility framework that lets programs inspect and

Windows and controls.

Scroll.
Zoom.
The application hosting the model is responsible for executing those actions and returning observations.

Action verification

                       │

Part 7 — Execution Environments
This is arguably the most important infrastructure decision in Stage 6.
Lain needs different environments for different types of work.
I recommend five execution backends.
Computer Use Engine:
    Executes click
Observation:
    Dialog remains open
Verification:
    Save operation may have failed
Agent:
    Investigates the failure
Backend Purpose Isolation
Native local Normal user-approved operations Limited
Restricted workspace Project development Filesystem/process restrictions
Take your curiosity further
Scan to get the ChatGPT app
https://chatgpt.com/g/g-p-6aecffda1c588191b6f066b657f38c50-lain/c/6aecf929-3068-83ea-9e72-66b7e13e53f5 187/352

===== PAGE 188 =====
188 / 352
1. Native local execution
This is the simplest backend.
It can:
Read and write authorized files.
Run commands.
Build projects.
Execute tests.
Interact with installed development tools.
But it should not be considered a strong security boundary.
An agent running commands under your normal Windows account may have access to more than its
intended project.
Therefore, filesystem restrictions and explicit permissions still matter.
2. Restricted workspace
This should be Lain's default development environment.
For example:
Backend Purpose Isolation
Container Reproducible builds and testing Container isolation
Virtual machine High-risk or incompatible workloads Stronger OS-level isolation
Remote worker Distributed workloads Separate machine/environment
LAIN
  │
  ├── Project A
  │     ├── Workspace
  │     ├── Terminal
  │     └── Git Worktree
Take your curiosity further
Scan to get the ChatGPT app
https://chatgpt.com/g/g-p-6aecffda1c588191b6f066b657f38c50-lain/c/6aecf929-3068-83ea-9e72-66b7e13e53f5 188/352

===== PAGE 189 =====
189 / 352
Each task receives a specific workspace.
Agents shouldn't automatically share writable directories.
This reduces accidental conflicts when several agents work simultaneously.
3. Containers
Containers are useful for:
Dependency isolation.
Build environments.
Reproducible testing.
Plugin testing.
Temporary development environments.
Controlled execution.
I'd evaluate Docker-compatible execution as an initial backend.
But don't assume a container automatically provides perfect security.
Lain should configure resource limits, restricted mounts, network policies, and execution permissions.
4. Virtual machines
For genuinely untrusted workloads, a VM provides stronger separation than simply running a process under
the user's normal account.
Your Windows 11 Pro environment has an interesting built-in option: Windows Sandbox.
  │
  ├── Project B
  │     ├── Workspace
  │     ├── Terminal
  │     └── Git Worktree
  │
  └── Project C
        ├── Workspace
        ├── Terminal
        └── Git Worktree

Scan to get the ChatGPT app
https://chatgpt.com/g/g-p-6aecffda1c588191b6f066b657f38c50-lain/c/6aecf929-3068-83ea-9e72-66b7e13e53f5 189/352

===== PAGE 190 =====
190 / 352
Microsoft describes it as a disposable, hardware-isolated environment. When closed, its temporary
software, files, and state are discarded. 
It supports configuration for:
Networking.
Mapped folders.
Read-only shared folders.
Memory allocation.
Virtual GPU.
Startup commands.
Networking is enabled by default, so it should be explicitly disabled when unnecessary. 
Hardware consideration: Windows Sandbox can be resource-intensive relative to your 16 GB RAM system.
I would not create a new Sandbox for every tiny operation.
Instead:
Use restricted workspaces for normal coding.
Use containers for routine isolated builds.
Reserve Sandbox/VM execution for higher-risk tasks.
Limit concurrent isolated environments.
5. Remote workers
Eventually, Lain should support workers running on other machines.
For example:

Part 8 — Autonomous Operations
This is where all the previous stages come together.
Lain should eventually be able to receive a goal, plan it, execute it, monitor progress, and recover from
failures.
But autonomy shouldn't mean unlimited authority.
I recommend dividing operations into six levels.
These levels build on the permission model from earlier stages.
Level Mode Behavior
0 Observe Inspect, research, analyze
1 Workspace Modify authorized project files
2 Execute Run approved commands and tests
3 Integrate Use approved external services
4 Autonomous Complete bounded multi-step tasks
5 Unattended Run approved recurring workflows within strict limits

===== PAGE 192 =====
192 / 352
An agent's autonomy should also depend on the task.
A developer agent might have permission to edit files and run tests, while a business agent may only have
permission to research and prepare materials.
